% Theorem D''': uniform-in-time propagation of chaos in the learning regime (the averaged slow loop)
% (PARADIGM.md, section 5d).
\section{Theorem D\texorpdfstring{$'''$}{'''}: uniform-in-time propagation of chaos in the learning regime (the
averaged slow loop)}\label{sec:thmDppp}

Theorem D$''$ ends where the leak stops dominating the loop gain: in the learning regime its small-gain argument has
nothing to say, and the long-horizon run of \code{exp_chaos_uniform.py} showed the coupled distance growing over the
horizon. This section isolates the structure that the small-gain argument throws away. Under the synchronous coupling
the interaction between the particles enters through the fitted function $\fhat_\Theta$, which is linear in the
empirical measure; the loss $\bar L$ is a convex quadratic of it (flat convexity, Theorem H(i)); and the interaction
term of the coupling identity is therefore the negative of a squared $L^2(\rho)$-norm at linear order --- it
contracts, whatever the gains. What remains and can expand is the self term, whose curvature is the Hessian of the
first-variation potential, $\sigma_0\Ginv - R[\mu](\vartheta)$ with $R$ the \emph{residual-weighted} curvature of the
features --- the same object as Proposition K's $R_2$ at a collapsed configuration. Proposition K says that the
collapsed configuration is unstable when this curvature exceeds the leak \emph{with the collapsed residual}; at a
fitted configuration the residual is the approximation error, and the second-order optimality condition of the limit
measure says that the curvature is \emph{below} the leak at every atom. The theorem below turns this into a
uniform-in-time statement for the averaged slow loop at $\tau = 0$, with explicit constants, in the regime in which
the network learns.

\paragraph{Setting.} The averaged slow dynamics of Section~\ref{sec:thmJprime} at $\tau_0 = 0$ (Theorem J$'$'s
leading order $\bar L_0$ without dither; slow time $s = t/k_e$, leak $\sigma_0 = k_e\sigma$, $f = f(x)$ continuous,
$\rho$ the occupation measure of the $T_0$-periodic reference, $F_\rho := \sup_{\supp\rho}\norm{f}$, saturated
features, $K_\Phi := \sup\norm{\tilde\Phi}$), written as the $G$-preconditioned gradient flow of
$J_m(\Theta) = m\bar L(\mu_\Theta) + \frac{\sigma_0}2\sum_i\norm{\vartheta_i - \vartheta^0}^2_{\Ginv}$:
\begin{gather}
  \frac{\dd\vartheta_i}{\dd s} = -G\nabla_{\vartheta_i}J_m(\Theta) = -G\nabla_\vartheta\mathcal{V}[\mu_\Theta](\vartheta_i),\qquad
  i = 1..m,
  \tag{$\mathfrak{A}_m$}\\
  \mathcal{V}[\mu](\vartheta) := V_L[\mu](\vartheta) + \frac{\sigma_0}2\norm{\vartheta - \vartheta^0}^2_{\Ginv},\notag
\end{gather}
with $V_L[\mu](\vartheta) = -\langle r_\mu^\T\tilde\Phi(\cdot\,;\vartheta)\rangle_\rho$ and $r_\mu = f - \fhat[\mu]$,
$\fhat[\mu] := \int\tilde\Phi(\cdot\,;\vartheta')\dd\mu(\vartheta')$, as in Section~\ref{sec:thmH}. Its mean-field
equation is the Wasserstein gradient flow $\Aavg$ of Section~\ref{sec:thmH} at $\tau = 0$ in slow time,
$\partial_s\mu_s = \nabla\cdot(\mu_sG\nabla\mathcal{V}[\mu_s])$, solved by the characteristic flow
\begin{equation}
  \frac{\dd X_s(\vartheta)}{\dd s} = -G\nabla\mathcal{V}[\mu_s](X_s(\vartheta)),\qquad X_0 = \mathrm{id},\qquad
  \mu_s = (X_s)_\#\mu_0
  \tag{$\mathfrak{A}_0$}
\end{equation}
(a McKean--Vlasov equation without noise whose vector field has linear growth through the leak, is Lipschitz in
$\vartheta$ and, through $r_\mu$, Lipschitz in $\mu$ for $W_1$; existence, uniqueness and the representation by
characteristics follow from Dobrushin's argument~\cite{S35} --- the synchronous-coupling proof of Theorem D$'$(b)
with the plant removed --- and the velocity is bounded on the invariant ball of Step~0). The constants are taken in
the $\Ginv$ metric on parameters (``whitened''):
\[
  L_\vartheta^G := \sup_{x,\vartheta}\norm{\nabla_\vartheta\tilde\Phi(x;\vartheta)G^{1/2}},\qquad
  L_2^G := \sup_{x,\vartheta,\norm{u}=1}\norm{G^{1/2}\nabla^2_\vartheta\bigl(u^\T\tilde\Phi(x;\vartheta)\bigr)G^{1/2}},
\]
so that $\norm{\tilde\Phi(x;\vartheta) - \tilde\Phi(x;\vartheta')}\le L_\vartheta^G\norm{\vartheta - \vartheta'}_{\Ginv}$,
$\norm{\nabla_\vartheta\tilde\Phi(x;\vartheta)v}\le L_\vartheta^G\norm{v}_{\Ginv}$ and
$\norm{(\nabla_\vartheta\tilde\Phi(x;\vartheta) - \nabla_\vartheta\tilde\Phi(x;\vartheta'))v}
\le L_2^G\norm{\vartheta - \vartheta'}_{\Ginv}\norm{v}_{\Ginv}$ ($L_\vartheta^G\le\lambda_G^{1/2}L_\vartheta$ and
$L_2^G\le\lambda_GL_2$ in the constants of Section~\ref{sec:thmDprime};
\code{chaos_learning.whitened_lipschitz_constants} computes the suprema by search, with closed-form bounds). The
\emph{residual-weighted curvature} of $\mu$ at $\vartheta$ is the symmetric matrix
\begin{gather*}
  R[\mu](\vartheta) := \Bigl\langle\sum_kr_\mu^k(x)\nabla^2_\vartheta\tilde\Phi^k(x;\vartheta)\Bigr\rangle_\rho
  = -\nabla^2_\vartheta V_L[\mu](\vartheta),\qquad R^G := G^{1/2}RG^{1/2},\\
  \kappa_R[\mu](A) := \sup_{\vartheta\in A}\lambda_{\max}\bigl(R^G[\mu](\vartheta)\bigr),
\end{gather*}
so that $G^{1/2}\nabla^2_\vartheta\mathcal{V}[\mu](\vartheta)G^{1/2} = \sigma_0I - R^G[\mu](\vartheta)$; for
$\mu = \delta_{\bar\vartheta}$ and $\vartheta = \bar\vartheta$ it is Proposition K's $R_2$ and
$\sigma_{\mathrm{crit}}(\bar\vartheta) = \kappa_R[\delta_{\bar\vartheta}](\{\bar\vartheta\})$. It satisfies
$\norm{R^G[\mu](\vartheta)}\le L_2^G\langle\norm{r_\mu}\rangle_\rho\le L_2^G(F_\rho + K_\Phi)$ and
$\norm{R^G[\mu](\vartheta) - R^G[\nu](\vartheta)}\le L_2^G\norm{\fhat[\mu] - \fhat[\nu]}_{L^1(\rho)}$.
$N_\varrho(A) := \{\vartheta : \operatorname{dist}_{\Ginv}(\vartheta,A)\le\varrho\}$.

\smallskip
\noindent\emph{The coupling.} $\vartheta_i(0)$, $i = 1..m$, i.i.d.\ with law $\mu_0$ of compact support,
$R_0 := \sup_{\supp\mu_0}\norm{\vartheta - \vartheta^0}_{\Ginv}$; $\Theta(s)$ solves \textup{($\mathfrak{A}_m$)} and
the reference particles are $\bar\vartheta_i(s) := X_s(\vartheta_i(0))$, i.i.d.\ with law $\mu_s$ and
$\bar\vartheta_i(s)\in\supp\mu_s$. Let $\delta_i := \vartheta_i - \bar\vartheta_i$,
\[
  D(s) := \frac1m\sum_i\norm{\delta_i(s)}^2_{\Ginv},\qquad
  Y(s) := \norm{\fhat_{\bar\Theta(s)} - \fhat[\mu_s]}_{L^2(\rho)},
\]
$Y$ being the sampling fluctuation of the reference's fitted function
($\E Y(s)^2 = \frac1m\langle\operatorname{Var}_{\mu_s}\tilde\Phi(x;\cdot)\rangle_\rho\le K_\Phi^2/m$). A priori
(Step~0 below) every particle of either system stays in the ball
$\norm{\vartheta - \vartheta^0}_{\Ginv}\le R_\Theta := \max\{R_0,\ L_\vartheta^G(F_\rho + K_\Phi)/\sigma_0\}$, so
$D\le D_{\max} := 4R_\Theta^2$, and the characteristics move at speed at most
$V_{\max} := L_\vartheta^G(F_\rho + K_\Phi) + \sigma_0R_\Theta$.

\begin{thm}{Theorem}{D$'''$}[uniform-in-time propagation of chaos in the learning regime, $\tau = 0$]
Assume
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=3em]
  \item[\textup{(H)}] \emph{(non-degenerate stability along the mean-field flow)} there are $s_1\ge0$, $\kappa > 0$
    and $\varrho' > 0$ such that
    \[
      \kappa_R[\mu_s]\bigl(N_{\varrho'}(\supp\mu_s)\bigr)\le\sigma_0 - \kappa\qquad\text{for every }s\ge s_1,
    \]
\end{itemize}
and put $a(s) := \sigma_0 - \kappa_R[\mu_s](N_{\varrho'}(\supp\mu_s))$ (so $a\ge\kappa$ on $[s_1,\infty)$ and
$a\ge\sigma_0 - L_2^G(F_\rho + K_\Phi)$ everywhere) and $\Lambda_1 := \int_0^{s_1}(\kappa - a(s))_+\dd s < \infty$.
Define
\begin{gather*}
  \varrho := \min\bigl\{(2\kappa)^{1/2}/L_2^G,\ \varrho'\kappa e^{-\Lambda_1}/(4(L_\vartheta^G)^2)\bigr\},\\
  \theta := \min\bigl\{\kappa/(8L_2^G),\ \varrho\kappa e^{-\Lambda_1}/(4L_\vartheta^G),\ \varrho'\kappa e^{-\Lambda_1}/(4L_\vartheta^G)\bigr\},\\
  \Delta := \theta/(4L_\vartheta^GV_{\max}),\qquad C_1 := 4e^{2\Lambda_1}(L_\vartheta^G)^2K_\Phi^2/\kappa^2,\qquad
  m_0 := 16K_\Phi^2/\theta^2.
\end{gather*}
Then for every $m\ge m_0$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
  \item \emph{(exponentially long horizons)} for every $S > 0$ and every $s\in[0,S]$,
    \[
      \E D(s)\le C_1/m + 4R_\Theta^2(S/\Delta + 2)\exp\bigl(-m\theta^2/(32K_\Phi^2)\bigr);
    \]
    in particular $\sup_{s\le S}\E D(s)\le(C_1 + 12R_\Theta^2)/m$ for all $S\le\Delta e^{m\theta^2/(64K_\Phi^2)}$ and
    $m\ge m_0$ with $e^{m\theta^2/(64K_\Phi^2)}\ge m$.
  \item \emph{(uniform in time)} if moreover \textup{(H$'$)} the characteristic flow converges exponentially on the
    support of $\mu_0$, $\sup_{\vartheta\in\supp\mu_0}\norm{X_s(\vartheta) - X_\infty(\vartheta)}_{\Ginv}\le C_2e^{-\kappa_2s}$
    for all $s\ge0$, then for every $s\ge0$
    \[
      \E D(s)\le C_1/m + 4R_\Theta^2(R_{\mathrm{eff}}/\Delta + 3)\exp\bigl(-m\theta^2/(32K_\Phi^2)\bigr),\qquad
      R_{\mathrm{eff}} := \kappa_2^{-1}\bigl(\log(8L_\vartheta^GC_2/\theta)\bigr)_+.
    \]
  \item \emph{(the learned function and the particles)} in either case, with the same right-hand side $B(s)$ for
    $\E D(s)$,
    \begin{gather*}
      \E\norm{\fhat_{\Theta(s)} - \fhat[\mu_s]}^2_{L^2(\rho)}\le2(L_\vartheta^G)^2B(s) + 2K_\Phi^2/m,\\
      \E W_2(\mu_{\Theta(s)},\mu_s)\le B(s)^{1/2} + \E W_2(\mu_{\bar\Theta(s)},\mu_s),
    \end{gather*}
    and on the event $\mathcal{G}_S := \{\sup_{s\le S}Y(s)\le\theta\}$ (case \textup{(a)}; $\mathcal{G}_\infty$ in
    case \textup{(b)}), whose complement is the tail above, every particle stays within $3\varrho'/4$ of its reference
    up to time $S$: $\max_i\sup_{s\le S}\norm{\delta_i(s)}_{\Ginv}\le3\varrho'/4$ --- no particle changes basin.
\end{enumerate}
The constants depend on $(\kappa,\varrho',\Lambda_1,L_\vartheta^G,L_2^G,K_\Phi,F_\rho,\sigma_0,R_0)$ and not on $m$,
$s$ or $S$.
\end{thm}
\status{Proved (synchronous coupling with the three-term identity of Step~1, a bootstrap on the mean square and the
per-particle distance, and McDiarmid's inequality~\cite{S36} for the reference's sampling fluctuation; the
finite-horizon part of the transient is Theorem D$'$'s mechanism and appears in $\Lambda_1$). Hypothesis (H) is a
property of the deterministic mean-field flow and can be computed; it is the strict second-order optimality condition
of the limit configuration with a margin and a neighbourhood (Remark D$'''$.1), and it is compatible with the learning
regime $\sigma_0 < \sigma_{\mathrm{crit}}$ of Proposition K. It requires the support of $\mu_s$ to stay away from the
ridges of $\mathcal{V}[\mu_s]$ for $s\ge s_1$: for an initial cloud whose support is a union of pieces each flowing
into one basin (a warm start, or a cluster per bump) this holds; for a connected cold start whose mass splits between
several basins it fails for every $s$ (Remark D$'''$.2), and the numerics below show what the coupling does there.
Numerics: \code{exp_chaos_learning.py} $\to$ \code{results/chaos/learning_table.md} (below). The theorem is about the
averaged slow dynamics; its transfer to the real loop at finite $k_e$ is open (Remark D$'''$.4). Its constants are
worst-case and its regime is asymptotic: on the instance below $m_0$ is of order $10^{23}$ (Remark D$'''$.6), and the
finite-$m$ behaviour is described sharply by Proposition D$'''$.5. Referee round 8
(\code{review/REVIEW_round8_Dppp.md}) re-derived every constant of Steps 0--9, found no counterexample to (2.1),
(3.1), (4.1) on an adversarial search, and corrected the wording of Remarks D$'''$.1--D$'''$.2, the good event per
horizon and the well-posedness sentence (all applied). Referee round 9 (\code{review/REVIEW_round9_D3b.md}) reviewed
Proposition D$'''$.5, Corollary D$'''$.7 and the numerics: all hold with fixes, the main one being that the finite-$m$
replicated configuration is a minimiser only for admissible weights (Proposition D$'''$.5(iv)) --- one of the four
$m = 16$ warm seeds sits on a saddle, which the numerics paragraph now says. Novelty (literature audit): the technique (synchronous
coupling + bootstrap + bounded differences) is standard; the sign structure of the interaction and the local,
residual-weighted hypothesis --- and with them a uniform-in-time statement in a regime where the network learns ---
are new in propagation of chaos for adaptive control; the theorem's regime is asymptotic in $m$ and the finite-$m$ law
is a delta-method statement, new in its object.}

\paragraph{Literature audit} (\code{LITERATURE_TRANSPORT.md}, D$'''$).
\begin{itemize}
  \item \emph{Prior.} Uniform-in-time propagation of chaos by synchronous coupling under \emph{global} convexity of
    the confinement and convexity of the interaction is Malrieu's~\cite{S37}; non-convex confinements with
    \emph{small} Lipschitz interactions are handled by reflection coupling~\cite{S38}; flat-convex energies with dither
    by the entropy method~\cite{S5}. The Hessian decomposition ``Gram + residual-weighted feature curvature'' is the
    standard tool of the interpolation\,/\,PL-condition literature~\cite{S39} and of lazy-training analyses, where the
    second term vanishes at interpolation.
  \item \emph{Distinction.} (i) The interaction of the fitted network is neither small nor convex in the particle
    coordinates; its sign comes from the flat convexity of $\bar L$ ($\fhat$ linear in the measure), which makes
    $-\norm{\fhat_\Theta - \fhat_{\bar\Theta}}^2$ the leading interaction term under the coupling --- the finite-$m$,
    two-measure version of \cite{S39}'s decomposition. (ii) The convexity needed is local:
    $\sigma_0I - G^{1/2}R[\mu_s]G^{1/2}\succeq\kappa I$ on a neighbourhood of the support of the mean-field flow,
    i.e.\ the strict second-order optimality condition of the fitted configuration, evaluated at the actual residual
    --- not a global convexity (which would force the collapsed configuration to be stable and the network not to
    learn, Theorem D$''$). (iii) The window $\kappa_R[\mu_\infty] < \sigma_0 < \sigma_{\mathrm{crit}}$ between the
    collapsed instability (Proposition K) and the fitted stability is a design statement about the leak that has no
    counterpart in the cited work. (iv) The limits are stated with the result: a connected initial support that splits
    between basins violates the hypothesis for all times (the characteristic flow is a homeomorphism), and the
    coupling then has a floor of pairs in different basins; the real loop at finite $k_e$ is not covered.
  \item \emph{Proposition D$'''$.5 (the weight-sensitivity law).} At finite $m$ the stationary coupled distance is the
    multinomial fluctuation of the cluster weights propagated through the implicit function $w\mapsto\Theta^*(w)$: a
    delta-method statement (the Gaussian limit of a smooth function of a multinomial), standard in statistics; what is
    specific is its object (the shared-particle coupling of a particle approximation to a stationary $K$-atom measure,
    with the covariance $(1/m - 1/M)(\operatorname{diag}\bar w - \bar w\bar w^\T)$) and its role as the sharp form of
    the theorem's constant (stiffness$^{-1}$ $\times$ feature correlations against $\kappa^{-1}L_\vartheta^GK_\Phi$).
  \item \emph{Proposition D$'''$.9 (measure-valued priors).} Leaks toward a single prior ($\sigma$-modification,
    Ioannou--Kokotovi\'c) are the standard of robust adaptive control; regularisation toward the nearest of several
    centres is standard in machine learning (mixture priors, prototype regularisers) and this document's own
    Theorem C$'$ leaks toward a prior \emph{measure} through an optimal plan. The nearest-atom leak is the simplest
    measure-valued prior; what is new is its role in the window of Theorem D$'''$ --- the prior's placement sets the
    stationary residual, hence the lower end of the window and the relaxation rate --- and the finding that removing
    the bias raises the coupling's floors (on the instance the weight sensitivity rises with the amplitudes): a
    bias--variance trade-off stated for the particle system. Verdict: standard object, new design statement; the
    function-level sensitivity law is the delta method once more.
  \item \emph{Proposition D$'''$.12 (the mass constraint).} Capacity-constrained and equal-mass quantisation
    through optimal plans, and semi-discrete optimal transport with its Laguerre cells, are classical
    (\cite{S19,S42}); the integrality of the transportation polytope and the forest structure of its vertices are
    textbook (\cite{S40}); $c$-cyclical monotonicity is the standard optimality criterion (\cite{S41}). What is
    new is the observation that the finite-$m$ floor of the mean-field limit of an adaptive law is carried by a
    conservation law of its leak (the cluster masses under the nearest-atom leak), and that a design of the leak
    --- the mass constraint of the OT plan --- breaks it: on the commensurate sizes the stationary measure of the
    particle system coincides with the mean-field one, and the finite-$m$ fluctuation of the plan is counted (at
    most $K_0 - 1$ split particles) rather than estimated. Propagation of chaos with an exactly $m$-independent
    stationary law is not in the literature we know of; the mean-field form of the barycentric leak for an atomic
    prior (the Laguerre-cell leak) was Section~\ref{sec:program}'s open item. Numerics: \code{exp_ot_leak.py}.
    Verdict: textbook ingredients, new mechanism and new object (a conservation law of the leak as the source of
    the finite-size floor, broken by the plan's mass constraint).
  \item \emph{Proposition D$'''$.17 (convergence of the OT dynamics).} The Kurdyka--{\L}ojasiewicz route to the
    convergence of (sub)gradient trajectories of definable functions is standard (\cite{S43,S44}); its application to
    an adaptive law whose energy is a semiconcave transport cost plus a smooth loss is a routine verification (the
    saturated Gaussian features are exponential-analytic, the plan is a minimum over finitely many vertices).
    Verdict: imported tool, routine verification; the content is that the mass-constrained law converges without a
    rate, which separates the qualitative question (settled) from Theorem D$'''$'s quantitative one (open for the OT
    dynamics).
  \item \emph{Proposition D$'''$.20 (the entropic OT leak).} Entropic OT, Sinkhorn's algorithm and barycentric
    mappings of entropic plans are standard (\cite{S45}), and so is the interpolation of the entropic cost between
    optimal transport and a kernel (MMD-type) discrepancy as $\varepsilon$ runs from 0 to $\infty$ (Sinkhorn
    divergences, \cite{S45}); size-constrained soft assignment by Sinkhorn (equipartition) is related prior art for a
    soft mass constraint. A critical temperature equal to twice the largest eigenvalue of a conditional covariance is
    deterministic annealing's (\cite{S46}), with the same roles --- the particles as the codevectors, the prior's
    atoms as the data; here the curvature of the fit enters, and the threshold is the largest generalised eigenvalue
    of the conditional covariance relative to $I - R^G/\sigma_0$. What is new is the soft mass constraint as an
    adaptive law's leak --- a smooth gradient flow between the OT leak and the $\sigma$-modification toward the
    prior's barycentre --- in which the conditional covariance of a collapsed class is an effect of its mass excess,
    and its consequence for propagation of chaos: the masses equalise only down to a critical excess, so the
    finite-size floor returns at large $m$ for every positive regularisation. Verdict: standard ingredients, new
    object and new consequence (on the commensurate sizes only the hard constraint is floor-free for every $m$).
  \item \emph{Theorem D$'''$.21 (a measure-level basin for the OT leak).} The Polyak--{\L}ojasiewicz inequality and
    its linear convergence are classical (\cite{S47}); in Wasserstein space {\L}ojasiewicz inequalities are known for
    $\lambda$-convex and displacement-convex functionals, and PL inequalities through optimal maps for some that are
    not geodesically convex --- barycentre functionals, the distance of uniform point clouds to a density
    (\cite{S48}); the leak alone, $\frac12W^2(\cdot,\nu)$, satisfies one with constant 2 (its metric slope is
    $W(\cdot,\nu)$; AGS~\cite[\S10.4]{ags2008}). The theorem's plan segments are the generalised geodesic from
    $\mu^*$ to $\mu_\Theta$ based at the prior (AGS~\cite[\S9.2]{ags2008}), along which $\frac12W^2(\cdot,\nu)$ is
    1-convex, and turning convexity along such a curve into a slope bound is AGS's device. The adaptive law's energy
    is semiconvex along generalised geodesics only with a negative constant (the loss's curvature at the actual
    residual), so a global argument is unavailable. What is new is the PL inequality along that geodesic for this
    energy --- the loss linearised exactly in the fitted function with the curvature taken at the stationary
    residual, where the fixed counts of the mass constraint make the geodesic a particle interpolation and the
    fitted function's increment the average of the per-particle increments: its constant is set by the
    residual-weighted curvature along the segments and does not depend on $m$ (sharp, $2\sigma_0$, at a matching
    prior); an $m$-independent energy basin, and uniqueness of the global minimum among empirical measures under a
    curvature hypothesis at the stationary residual; and the converse for the nearest-atom leak, whose energy has a
    spurious minimum at every admissible count vector --- the energy-landscape form of the finite-size floor.
    Numerics: \code{exp_ot_basin.py}. Verdict: classical inequality and geometry (the generalised geodesic), new
    mechanism for a new energy (the counts as the source of uniformity in $m$).
  % The items Theorem D'''.23 to Corollary D'''.33 are LITERATURE_TRANSPORT.md's audit bullets as committed at 1ddff8c
  % (D'''.25 has none), the item Proposition D'''.34 as committed at 9d122dd (manuscript part 4g; the D'''.32 item is
  % unchanged there), the item Proposition D'''.35 as committed at 2e5eb10 (manuscript part 4h), the items
  % Propositions D'''.36 and D'''.37 as committed at c054367 and 5952611 (manuscript part 4i), the items
  % Propositions D'''.38 and D'''.39 as committed at 5d14f92 and 5b747b9 (manuscript part 4j), the items
  % Propositions D'''.41 to D'''.43 and D'''.45 to D'''.48 as committed at da1a80e and 1269272 (manuscript part 4k;
  % Propositions D'''.40 and D'''.44 are not yet in the audit). Its references
  % [S..] are the bibitems S.. of refs.tex; [1], [3], [4], [6]--[8], [30], [49]
  % and [55] (review/LITERATURE_refs.md) are sanner1992, x-ioannou1983 (Ioannou--Kokotovic's sigma-modification),
  % ioannou1996, R6--R8, R30, R49 and R55; [S9] (the standard references) is sb1989 where it is cited for
  % Sastry--Bodson and kko1986 where it is cited for Kokotovic--Khalil--O'Reilly and singular perturbations.
  \item \emph{Theorem D$'''$.23 (adaptive priors).} A quadratic penalty toward a centre that is moved toward the
    iterate is the proximal point method (iterated Tikhonov regularisation) and, in continuous time with a centre
    drawn to the mean of several coupled variables, elastic averaging (\cite{S49}); a learned mixture prior on the
    weights is soft weight-sharing, whose hard limit is a $K$-means regulariser (\cite{S49}), and the
    capacity-constrained form of that limit is \cite{S19}'s. The per-mode formulas are the classical quadratic
    analyses: $\sigma_0/(h_j + \sigma_0)$, the complement of the Tikhonov filter factor $h_j/(h_j + \sigma_0)$ (the
    residual factor), iterated Tikhonov's contraction by it per step (Hanke--Groetsch, \cite{S49}; in continuous time
    the gradient flow of the Moreau envelope, which is the slow-prior limit $\eta h_j/(h_j + \sigma_0)$ of the rate),
    and elastic averaging's worker--centre analysis on a quadratic, whose continuous-time form is the pair matrix
    $\left[\begin{smallmatrix}-(h + \sigma_0) & \sigma_0\\ \eta & -\eta\end{smallmatrix}\right]$; empirical Bayes for
    the mean of a Gaussian prior is the same proximal iteration. What is new is their transfer to the mass-constrained
    particle system of the OT leak --- the prior's atoms learned, its masses kept: $H_L$ identified as the
    measure-space excitation spectrum, the separations decoupled at $\sigma_0$, and hence the trade-off between the
    fixed prior's bias $\sigma_0/(h_j + \sigma_0)$ and the adaptive prior's rate
    $\eta h_j/(h_j + \sigma_0 + \eta) < \lambda_j^- < \min\{h_j,\eta\}$ uniformly in $m$ --- the rate half of the
    classical trade-off between a leak's robustness and persistent excitation, whose robustness half (the leak's
    a-priori bound) the adaptive prior gives up (\hyperref[res:thmDppp:27]{Proposition D$'''$.27} restores it by
    projecting the prior); and the transit's drag, with the gate that starts the adaptation from the fixed-prior
    stationary point. The limits of the bias removal are largely the precedents' own properties --- the lost a-priori
    bound is the proximal point method's behaviour without a minimiser (\cite{S49}), the $K_0$-cluster bias the
    quantisation error of soft weight-sharing's hard limit --- and specific here only in their particle,
    mass-constrained form and in the run-off from a fit that the network attains exactly. Numerics:
    \code{exp_adaptive_prior.py}. Verdict: standard ingredients and per-mode formulas (proximal point and iterated
    Tikhonov, elastic averaging, soft weight-sharing), a new mechanism (their transfer to the mass-constrained particle
    system, uniform in $m$) and a new failure mode with its remedy (the transit's drag and the gate).
  \item \emph{Proposition D$'''$.24 (the floor is the leak's bias).} The $m^{-1}$-size fluctuations of trained
    mean-field networks are classical (\cite{R30,S51}) and Proposition D$'''$.5/D$'''$.9's floor law is the delta
    method; the amplitude--mass degeneracy is the homogeneity of the conic view (\cite{R49,R55}); semi-relaxed
    transport (free target masses) reducing to the nearest-centre assignment is elementary. That the fluctuations'
    asymptotic deviation vanishes at an interpolating limit is Chen et al.'s (\cite{S51}); the derivation of $F$ is
    the chain rule applied to \hyperref[res:thmDppp:23]{Theorem D$'''$.23}(iii)'s first-order bias at $x^0(q) = 0$,
    and the weight $h_j\sigma_0^2/(h_j + \sigma_0)^2$ is the squared complement of the Tikhonov filter factor times
    the mode's visibility, bounded by ridge regression's AM--GM bound. What is new is the identification of the
    function-level floor of a regularised adaptive law with the weight-derivative of the regulariser's bias at the
    sampled weights --- at a matching prior $F = \hat J\sigma_0(H_L + \sigma_0I)^{-1}\partial_wx^0$, decomposed by
    modes with the band-pass weight, whose bound is $\sigma_0/4$ times the adaptive prior's coupled-distance law ---
    the split of the sample's weight fluctuation between the parameters ($h_j/(h_j + \sigma_0)$) and the bias
    ($\sigma_0/(h_j + \sigma_0)$), and a leak toward a learned prior (the OT leak with the masses minimised out) as
    the way to restore the interpolation property under regularisation, which also removes the fixed prior's
    splitting instability; the trade-off is a larger coupled distance of the gauge, the leak's a-priori bound
    (restored by projecting the prior, \hyperref[res:thmDppp:27]{Proposition D$'''$.27}) and the collective rate. On
    the instance the law (B) is asymptotic (the exact floor 2.8 times it at $m = 15$), and the samples without an
    exact representation are not rare at small $m$ (0.36 at $m = 15$). Numerics: \code{exp_adaptive_floor.py}.
    Verdict: classical ingredients (the delta method, the Gauss--Newton spectrum, semi-relaxed transport, the
    filter-factor bound, the interpolation mechanism), new mechanism (the floor as the bias's slope, mode by mode, and
    its removal by a learned prior under regularisation) and a resolution of Remark D$'''$.10's bias--variance
    trade-off at the function level, at the price of the collective rate.
  \item \emph{Corollary D$'''$.26 (perfect tracking with an adaptive prior: the Floquet spectrum).} Floquet theory and
    the averaging of a periodic variational equation are classical (first- and second-order averaging; the Magnus
    expansion, whose second term is a commutator, so that for a self-adjoint generator it is skew \cite{S52}); the
    averaging analysis of adaptive loops, with the rate set by the averaged regressor's persistency of excitation, is
    Kosut--Anderson--Mareels' and Sastry--Bodson's (\cite{S52}, \cite{sb1989}); asymptotic tracking under exact
    parameterisation is the textbook goal of adaptive control, which $\sigma$-modification gives up for robustness
    (\cite{x-ioannou1983}, \cite{ioannou1996}), and leaks compatible with it are known --- the $e$-modification scales
    the leak by $\abs{e}$ and vanishes at perfect tracking (\cite{S52}), a centre that follows the parameters has
    unbiased fixed points (\cite{S49}). (a) and (b) are Corollary D$'''$.14(ii) and Proposition D$'''$.15 at zero
    residual, with the prior's coordinates added; that an expansion is even when the problem is invariant under a
    symmetry reversing the small parameter's sign is the classical mechanism of the even error expansions of
    symmetric integrators and of the time symmetry of the Magnus exponent (\cite{S56}, \cite{S52}). What is specific
    here is the combination: with the adaptive prior the zero-residual lift exists at every representable sample (not
    only at $w = q$ under the OT leak), its Floquet spectrum is exactly $m$-independent with collective rates
    $\lambda_j^\pm(w)/k_e$, and its expansion in $1/k_e$ has an explicit second-order coefficient --- the error
    filter's attenuation against the slaving's speed-up, its sign not fixed (positive coefficients appear when the
    adaptation gain is raised) --- which a derivative mismatch $\partial_x(f - \fhat)\ne0$ would spoil at first order.
    Numerics: \code{exp_adaptive_floquet.py}. Verdict: classical tools, a new structural statement (an exact,
    $m$-independent spectrum at every representable sample, with the explicit second-order coefficient of this loop).
  \item \emph{Lemma D$'''$.26a (the exact slow subspace).} Chang's decoupling of linear singularly perturbed systems
    --- the transformation pair that block-diagonalises the system into its slow and fast parts --- is classical
    (\cite{S59}, \cite{kko1986}), for time-varying systems by a contraction (Yang--Zhu, \cite{S59}), with white-noise
    inputs and the slow subsystem's transformed noise and covariance (Kokotovi\'c--Khalil--O'Reilly Ch.~4,
    Haddad--Kokotovi\'c, \cite{S59}); stochastic slow manifolds and their covariance are Berglund and Gentz's
    (\cite{S59}); the convergence of the Magnus series is Moan and Niesen's (\cite{S59}); the evenness of an expansion
    under a symmetry reversing the small parameter is the mechanism of symmetric integrators, and
    $\Phi_K(-\varepsilon) = \Phi_{\tilde K}(\varepsilon)^{-1}$ is their adjoint relation (\cite{S56}). What is
    specific is the application to the perfect-tracking linearisation of the transport-leak loop: the slaving of
    \hyperref[res:thmDppp:26]{Corollary D$'''$.26} becomes an exact block-triangularisation, so the slow multipliers
    are exactly the slow block's; the parity becomes the exact identity
    $\operatorname{eig}\bar A(-\varepsilon) = \operatorname{eig}\bar A(\varepsilon)$, the slow block at negative
    $\varepsilon$ defined by the same (anti-causal) contraction; and the cancellation
    $K^\T - (1 + \varepsilon a)(K^\T + \varepsilon\dot K^\T) = -\varepsilon(\dot K + aK)^\T$ makes the slow noise of
    \hyperref[res:thmDppp:30]{Proposition D$'''$.30} $\varepsilon b\nu$, at leading order the high-passed
    $-\varepsilon(\dot K + aK)^\T\nu$ (the drift decoupled exactly, the noise shared with the fast part). Numerics:
    \code{exp_adaptive_floquet.py} Part D. Verdict: classical tools; a rigorous foundation for D$'''$.26(c) and
    D$'''$.30(ii) (the referee round 22's audit: fair once the stochastic and time-varying precedents are named).
  \item \emph{Proposition D$'''$.27 (a projected adaptive prior).} Projection of the parameter estimates onto a convex
    set (Pomet--Praly), the exterior quadratic penalty and the switching $\sigma$-modification are the classical
    robustness modifications of adaptive laws (\cite{ioannou1996}), and the switching $\sigma$-modification keeps the
    ideal (unbiased) properties of the law when the true parameter lies in its set (\cite{ioannou1996}, \cite{S52});
    the a-priori bound by a projection inequality and a comparison argument is standard. On collapsed classes at
    $\eta = \infty$ the construction is exactly that law, toward the set, with the leak off on it. What is specific
    here is its transfer to the learned centre of a mass-constrained $K_0$-atom prior at finite $\eta$ --- a projected
    adaptive law for the prior's atoms (whose velocity jumps at the faces, while the particles' law keeps its smooth
    gradient form) --- and its compatibility with \hyperref[res:thmDppp:23]{Theorem D$'''$.23}'s bias removal
    wherever the target is representable inside the set; at $\eta = \infty$ the plan is Lloyd-consistent rather than
    jointly optimal (as in Lloyd's algorithm for $K$-means, whose fixed points are local), so the monotone quantity is
    the selected energy, and Proposition D$'''$.17's convergence argument does not apply as it stands. Numerics:
    \code{exp_adaptive_prior.py} Part H. Verdict: classical ingredients and, at $\eta = \infty$, a classical law; a new
    transfer (the learned centre of the mass-constrained prior at finite $\eta$) that restores the robustness half of
    the trade-off which the adaptive prior gave up.
  \item \emph{Proposition D$'''$.28 (the floor removal is a gauge property).} The amplitude--mass degeneracy is the
    positive homogeneity that the conic (Wasserstein--Fisher--Rao) view of mean-field networks exploits
    (\cite{R49,R55}), and rescaling symmetries of network parameters --- which map critical points to critical points
    with the same function while changing their curvature --- are classical (\cite{S55}); the implicit-function
    derivation of the fixed prior's sensitivity is the one of \hyperref[res:thmDppp:24]{Proposition D$'''$.24}(i).
    What is specific here is that the gauge, not exact representability, is the mechanism of the adaptive prior's
    floor removal --- so it survives an approximation error, the regime of real plants, at a strict local minimum in
    the interior --- together with the exact scaling $R_k(G_w\vartheta) = (q_k/w_k)R_k(\vartheta)$ of the residual
    curvature at a critical point for a scalar amplitude per kernel, which turns the adaptive prior's splitting
    margins into a closed formula: the scalar-amplitude instance of the reparametrisation dependence of curvature
    \cite{S55}. Numerics: \code{exp_gauge_floor.py}. Verdict: classical symmetry, a new identification of the
    mechanism and an exact margin formula for a scalar amplitude.
  \item \emph{Proposition D$'''$.29 (second-order plants).} The sliding-variable (filtered-error) construction is the
    classical route of adaptive control to higher-order plants, and Gaussian networks with it are Sanner and Slotine's
    (\cite{sanner1992}); singular-perturbation averaging with a stable fast linear subsystem is standard
    (\cite{kko1986}); the exact filter $(\mathrm{d}/\mathrm{d}t + k_s)^{-1}$ in the position-error prediction is the
    filtered error itself, and the weak excitation of Gaussian networks along an orbit is the classical partial
    persistence of excitation (\cite{R6,R7,R8}). What is specific here is the transfer of the transport leaks of
    Section~\ref{sec:thmDppp} --- their finite-size floors, the mass constraint, the adaptive and projected priors and
    the gauge --- to such plants through the occupation measure of the regressor's orbit, and the exact fibre of
    isotropic phase-plane kernels on a circular orbit (von Mises restrictions: in general position one zero
    Gauss--Newton eigenvalue per kernel). The numerics are noise-free: the leaks' robustness is not exercised there,
    and no leak at all fits as well. Numerics: \code{exp_servo_cogging.py}. Verdict: a classical reduction; new
    applications of Section~\ref{sec:thmDppp}'s mechanisms to a standard servo problem, whose case rests on the noisy
    setting still to be run.
  \item \emph{Proposition D$'''$.30 (measurement noise at perfect tracking).} Measurement noise in adaptive control,
    the robust modifications against bounded disturbances (\cite{ioannou1996}) and the errors-in-variables bias of
    estimates from noisy regressors (bias compensation and instrumental variables in identification, \cite{S53}) are
    classical, and so is the remedy of putting the reference in the regressor (the desired compensation adaptation
    law, \cite{S53}); stochastic averaging is Khas'minskii's (\cite{S53}). So are the mechanisms behind the floor and
    the amplification (\cite{S57}): the Lyapunov covariance as the diffusion approximation of a constant-gain
    algorithm (Benveniste--M\'etivier--Priouret), a misadjustment depending on the input's spectrum only through a
    trace (Widrow et al.: the $\eta = \infty$ floor's $\tr Q$), the leaky filter's steady state (Mayyas--Aboulnasr:
    the fixed prior's factor $h_j/(h_j + \sigma_0)$), a common noise not averaging out in the mean-field limit
    (Carmona--Delarue--Lacker: the coherence) and the drift of weakly excited directions under a persistent
    disturbance (Anderson: the $H^{-1}$ response). What is specific here is their combination in the transport-leak
    setting: the floor's interpolation $(h_j + \eta)/(h_j + \eta + \sigma_0)$ in the prior's mobility (an elementary
    $2\times2$ Lyapunov computation), the driving matrix $Q = \langle(\dot{\hat J} + a\hat J)^\T(\dot{\hat J} + a\hat J)\rangle$
    of the sensitivity-filtered measurement the law sees (built on the derivative Gram of
    \hyperref[res:thmDppp:26]{Corollary D$'''$.26}'s filter correction), and the coherence of the $K_0$-atom
    identification with its noise threshold at the lift (invariance, not attraction; along the noisy trajectory the
    separations' drift grows with the developing errors-in-variables deviation, and the long-time threshold is open).
    Numerics: \code{exp_measurement_noise.py}. Verdict: classical ingredients; a quantified structural statement for
    the transport leaks under noise.
  \item \emph{Proposition D$'''$.31 (the washout prior and the desired regressor).} Washout filters --- feedback
    through a high-pass filter, which leaves the open loop's equilibria where they are and therefore cannot oppose a
    persistent input --- are classical in flight control and in bifurcation control (\cite{S54}); an adaptive law
    kept near a low-pass filtered copy of itself is Yucelen and Haddad's low-frequency learning (\cite{S58}), which
    has the washout structure, and a centre moving with its workers' average is elastic averaging's (\cite{S49}); the
    desired compensation adaptation law, whose regressor and compensation use the reference trajectory, is Sadegh and
    Horowitz's (\cite{S53}; its nonlinear damping, which DR* omits, is for global stability), and its regressor's
    freedom from measurement noise is part of its classical motivation. That a quadratic regularisation's stationary
    response and its bias toward the anchor sum to the identity --- $RH + B = I$, per mode the Tikhonov filter factor
    plus the residual factor, the Kalman-gain split of a regularised estimate --- is elementary, as are the
    minimum-norm bound (iii) and the split of a force into the regressor's errors in variables, which bias, and the
    output's, of which only the mean biases. What is specific here is their transfer to the transport leaks: the
    reading of \hyperref[res:thmDppp:23]{Theorem D$'''$.23}'s adaptive prior as a washout $\sigma$-modification of
    the class barycentres (for integral plans; split rows leave a non-washed-out part), uniform in $m$, and the
    quantification on the instance. The parameters' own errors in variables is the classical second-order bias of a
    constant-gain stochastic approximation from its own fluctuation (Benveniste--M\'etivier--Priouret;
    Dieuleveut--Durmus--Bach for constant-step SGD; Box's curvature bias of nonlinear least squares: \cite{S57}); what
    is added is its quantification here --- small in absolute terms ($\le3.9\cdot10^{-4}$ per $(\E\nu^2)^2$),
    $-8.7\%$ to $+3.3\%$ of DR's and DR*'s biases and $+56\%$ of DR's at $k_e = B = 100$, where DR's noise part
    nearly cancels; its own response falling like $k_e^{-2}$ on the one-dimensional reference, where the law's
    leading term averages out --- and the observation that the desired regressor does not remove it. Numerics:
    \code{exp_eiv_remedy.py}. Verdict: classical ingredients; a structural reading of the adaptive prior and a
    quantified, partial remedy for the transport leaks.
  \item \emph{Proposition D$'''$.32 (the cohesion leak).} The leak is the mean-shift step of the parameters' own
    kernel density (Fukunaga--Hostetler, Cheng: mode seeking with a shadow kernel), so its mean-field form is an
    aggregation equation in the fit's external potential with the fit's and the cohesion's interactions --- a
    Wasserstein gradient flow of an interaction energy (Carrillo--McCann--Villani, Bertozzi--Laurent--Rosado);
    particles drifting toward their own kernel-weighted local means are polarized consensus-based dynamics
    (Bungert--Roith--Wacker), and clustering under softmax-weighted attraction is the self-attention dynamics of
    Geshkovski--Letrouit--Polyanskiy--Rigollet; agents averaging within a confidence radius form clusters at least the
    radius apart (Hegselmann--Krause), which is what the finite range does to the classes; a duplicated hidden unit
    turns a critical point into a line of critical points (Fukumizu--Amari), the twins' curves of (v); a neuron split
    along the top eigenvector of its residual-weighted Hessian is splitting steepest descent (Wu--Wang--Liu); a
    prior-free pairwise attraction whose strength trades the fit against the number of distinct parameters is convex
    clustering and sum-of-norms identification (Hocking--Joulin--Bach--Vert, Ohlsson--Ljung--Boyd), and a cluster
    splitting when an annealed parameter crosses a value set by its covariance is deterministic annealing (Rose), the
    structure of (iv)'s resolution and of the annealed scale; RBF units added and removed by accuracy criteria are
    self-organising RBF networks (Lian et al.); forgetting only in the excited directions is directional forgetting
    (Kulhav\'y), and forgetting toward a prior on the unexcited ones stabilized forgetting (Kulhav\'y--Zarrop); the
    extra modes of an isotropic Gaussian mixture need overlapping components (Carreira-Perpi\~n\'an--Williams), which
    (v) uses (\cite{S60}). What is specific here is the combination as a leak in mean-field adaptive control: the
    exact blocks at clustered configurations with the mass factor $\sigma_0w_k$, the gauge-scaled splitting margin
    $\sigma_0w_k - (q_k/w_k)\lambda^+_k$, the twins' contraction rate, and the real loop's periodic lift with the
    separation exponents $\sigma_0w_k/k_e$. The number of kernels is not selected by the leak (Numerics (C), (J),
    (K)). Numerics: \code{exp_cohesion_leak.py}. Verdict: classical ingredients; a new prior-free leak on the
    unexcited subspace.
  \item \emph{Corollary D$'''$.33 (the desired regressor and the coherence under noise).} That a regressor evaluated
    on the reference trajectory removes the measurement noise from the adaptation's regressor is part of the
    classical motivation of the desired compensation adaptation law (Sadegh and Horowitz, \cite{S53}). The
    corollary's question --- whether identical subsystems driven by a common noise stay together --- is that of
    noise-induced synchronisation: identical uncoupled oscillators driven by a common weak noise synchronise when the
    transverse Lyapunov exponent, to second order in the noise, is negative, and a strong noise can make it positive
    (Teramae--Tanaka, Goldobin--Pikovsky; with independent noise, clustering: Nakao--Arai--Kawamura; \cite{S61}).
    \hyperref[res:thmDppp:30]{Proposition D$'''$.30}(i)'s threshold and this corollary are that exponent for the
    replicated kernels, the leak their transverse stiffness, the variance term the exponent's own second-order
    correction. That the regressor's term leaves the separations' drift is the one-line consequence of a regressor
    that does not see the measurement. What is specific here: that consequence for the $K_0$-atom identification ---
    the drift $O(1/k_e)$ like the stiffness, a threshold bounded below uniformly in $k_e$ and constant when the
    noise's bandwidth scales with the gain --- and its quantification on the instance: the sign at the lift (negative
    in the centre directions of separated kernels; the whole matrix only for separated alternating-sign kernels), its
    loss along the trajectory as the mean error relaxes with the collective modes, and the stationary point, where the
    parameters' part of the force dominates the mean error. Numerics: \code{exp_eiv_remedy.py} Part D. Verdict: a
    classical remedy in a classical frame; a new structural consequence for the transport leaks (and for the cohesion
    leak, whose separations are the softest).
  \item \emph{Proposition D$'''$.34 (mass transfer: the replicator weight law).} Transport and creation of mass
    together are the Wasserstein--Fisher--Rao (Hellinger--Kantorovich) geometry of positive measures
    (Liero--Mielke--Savar\'e); particles that move and change weight are conic particle gradient descent, with global
    convergence for sparse optimisation on measures under non-degeneracy (Chizat), and a non-local birth--death term
    added to the mean-field dynamics of a one-hidden-layer network keeps the minimiser and speeds the convergence
    (Rotskoff--Jelassi--Bruna--Vanden-Eijnden); the multiplicative weight update is the exponentiated-gradient law
    (Kivinen--Warmuth) and EM's update of mixture weights (Dempster--Laird--Rubin), adaptation laws of mirror-descent
    type replace the quadratic parameter error of the Lyapunov function by a Bregman divergence (Boffi--Slotine) ---
    (ii) is their negative-entropy instance ---, and the replicator equation is the gradient flow of Shahshahani's
    metric with the relative entropy as its Lyapunov function at a stable state (Shahshahani, Hofbauer--Sigmund); the
    Wasserstein--Fisher--Rao distance has two more origins (Kondratyev--Monsaingeon--Vorotnikov,
    Chizat--Peyr\'e--Schmitzer--Vialard), and gradient descent on the positions and square-root weights of a
    two-layer network is its particle form (Chizat--Bach) (\cite{S62}). The law here is gradient descent on
    $(\sqrt\beta,\Theta)$ --- Euclidean on the positions, not the WFR metric's mass-weighted one ---, with the
    cohesion energy of the unweighted cloud. What is specific: the replicator as the mass transfer of the cohesion
    leak in mean-field adaptive control --- the stationary set unchanged at clustered configurations under the finite
    range (under the Gaussian kernel the replicator selects the masses), the saturation's bound kept without a
    projection, the saturation trap of the transit's masses removed, the representability criterion
    $\sum_k\abs{A_k}/a_{\max} < 1$ at fixed positions for bounded trajectories, the zero modes at the lift --- and the
    numerics (the five-bump and three-heavy-bump targets found with their kernels; bumps closer than the broad scale
    and the shedding not repaired). Numerics: \code{exp_mass_transfer.py}. Verdict: classical ingredients; a new
    remedy for the transit's masses.
  \item \emph{Proposition D$'''$.35 (the split as an $S_n$-symmetric transcritical bifurcation).} Bifurcation from a
    fully symmetric state of $N$ identical coupled units is $S_N$-equivariant; the standard representation admits a
    quadratic equivariant, so the generic bifurcation is transcritical, with branches of isotropy $S_q\times S_{N-q}$
    and no stable small branch --- the jump of the speciation models of Stewart--Elmhirst--Cohen and
    Golubitsky--Stewart, the normal forms and stability of Elmhirst, the generic theory with quadratic equivariants of
    Arjevani--Field, who motivate it by the permutation-symmetric critical points of two-layer networks; duplicated
    units give such critical points and saddles (Fukumizu--Amari \cite{S60}, Brea et al., \c{S}im\c{s}ek et al.)
    (\cite{S63}); a cluster splits at a critical parameter set by its covariance in deterministic annealing (Rose,
    \cite{S60}). Order preservation is a consequence of the equivariance, and the escape time is the Riccati
    solution. What is specific here: the identification for the cohesion leak --- the quadratic coefficient the
    residual's third derivative along the splitting direction, the cohesion and the mean term entering only at third
    order or in the common direction ---, the margin after a departure (the stiffness's drop and the curvature's gauge
    rise), and the instance's quantification (the extreme particle leaving first, alone or with a near-tied
    neighbour; the mass-free leak delaying the cascade without stopping it). Numerics:
    \code{exp_split_bifurcation.py}. Verdict: classical bifurcation theory; a new identification for the cohesion
    leak's over-splitting.
  \item \emph{Proposition D$'''$.36 (the gauge under dither).} A diffusion pushed onto a
    manifold of equilibria by a large drift converges, on the slow time scale, to a diffusion on the manifold whose
    noise is the original projected by the derivative of the flow's limit map (Katzenberger \cite{S64}); the
    noise-driven motion of SGD along the manifold of minimisers of an overparameterised model is the same limit (Blanc
    et al.; Li--Wang--Arora \cite{S64}), and the constructive projection is Parsons--Rogers'. The entropic leak on the
    weights is the negative-entropy mirror map's regulariser (\cite{S62}). What is specific here: the gauge of the
    amplitude--mass representation (\hyperref[res:thmDppp:28]{Proposition D$'''$.28}) as such a manifold for the
    replicator of \hyperref[res:thmDppp:34]{Proposition D$'''$.34}; the projection computed in the flow's own metric
    (Euclidean--$G^{-1}$ on the positions, Fisher--Rao on the masses), with the two-class closed form; the excursion
    into the saturation, where the limit map's derivative degenerates; and the entropic leak on the masses as a leak
    on the unexcited subspace, which pins the gauge without bias where the particle fractions are representable.
    Numerics: \code{exp_gauge_dither.py}. Verdict: a classical limit theorem applied; the replicator's unpinned masses
    under dither, and their remedy.
  \item \emph{Proposition D$'''$.37 (the dither's drift along the gauge).} The second
    derivative of the limit map and the drift it gives a diffusion forced onto a manifold of equilibria are
    Katzenberger's; for isotropic noise the drift along a manifold of minima is $-\frac12$ of the gradient of the
    log-pseudo-determinant of the Hessian (Li--Wang--Arora's Corollary 5.1, \cite{S64}; checked by round 27's referee
    on its summary only); the averaging of a slow variable over a fast ergodic one is Khasminskii's, and a slow
    coordinate in equilibrium with fast ones feels their free energy, the potential of mean force (\cite{S65}); that
    noisy gradient dynamics prefers flat minima is the flat-minima picture of Hochreiter--Schmidhuber, and Entropy-SGD
    makes the local entropy the objective (\cite{S65}). Not seen, named by the referee: Fixman's and
    Bornemann--Sch\"utte's corrections for stiff constraints, the label-noise analyses of Damian--Ma--Lee and HaoChen
    et al., noisy replicator dynamics (Fudenberg--Harris, Foster--Young), Rose's deterministic annealing. What is
    specific here: the replicator's masses as the slow variable on the amplitude--mass gauge, with noise on the
    positions only, and its free energy in closed form ($2\sum\log(W_ka'_k)$); the consequence that dither drives the
    masses into the saturation --- the trap the replicator removed (\hyperref[res:thmDppp:34]{Proposition D$'''$.34})
    returning through the noise ---; the boundary layer of width $O(\sqrt\gamma)$, where the replicator outruns the
    saturating amplitude, which slows but does not stop it; and the entropic leak's bias $\kappa^{-1}b(w)$. Numerics:
    \code{exp_gauge_drift.py}. Verdict: classical limits (Katzenberger, Li--Wang--Arora, averaging) applied; a new
    mechanism for the replicator's gauge under dither.
  \item \emph{Proposition D$'''$.38 (a Fixman correction for the replicator under dither).} That a stiff system's free
    energy along its soft directions carries a log-determinant, and that adding the opposite term makes the
    constrained dynamics sample as intended, is Fixman's (the log-det term the stiff limit's zero-point term, Helfand;
    disputed in the overdamped setting by Waszkiewicz--Lisicki); the drift corrections between stiff bonds and rigid
    constraints are Hinch's, and the projected diffusions and the mean force on submanifolds are
    Ciccotti--Leli\`evre--Vanden-Eijnden's (\cite{S66}); removing a free-energy drift online is the adaptive biasing
    force method, a gain on it well-tempered metadynamics' bias factor, and the max-det maximiser of a log-barrier is
    classical in convex optimisation (\cite{S66}); the log-determinant drift itself is Li--Wang--Arora's for isotropic
    noise (\cite{S64}) and \hyperref[res:thmDppp:37]{Proposition D$'''$.37}'s for the replicator. What is specific
    here: the correction for the replicator's gauge, applied online to the masses only, in closed form on the masses
    --- a log-barrier on the representable masses, the corrected loop a gradient flow --- and the distinction between
    that masses form and the form at the current amplitudes, which reads the positions' escapes off the manifold as
    masses at their saturation; the selection of the D-optimal exact fit $W^\sharp$ with the gain and the confinement
    exponent $\delta^{\kappa_F - 1}$ are applications; the leak's pinned state predicted to second order (the
    fluctuations through the leak's curvature, which the paired seeds resolve). Numerics: \code{exp_gauge_fixman.py}.
    Verdict: a classical correction carried to a new setting, with a new design knob.
  \item \emph{Proposition D$'''$.39 (dissipativity of the dithered gauge).} That a Langevin dynamics needs a confining
    potential to have an invariant probability law is classical (Mattingly--Stuart--Higham; in learning,
    Raginsky--Rakhlin--Telgarsky's dissipativity), as are the exit times' exponential order (Freidlin--Wentzell;
    sharp, Bovier--Eckhoff--Gayrard--Klein) and parameter projection with its Lipschitz boundary layer (Ioannou--Sun,
    Pomet--Praly), and the dead zone itself is the Moreau--Yosida envelope of the box's indicator, with which MYULA
    samples on a convex set (Durmus--Moulines--Pereyra; Brosse et al.) --- a switching $\sigma$-modification in
    adaptive control (\cite{S67}). What is specific here: the cohesion leak's removal of the prior
    (\hyperref[res:thmDppp:32]{Proposition D$'''$.32}) removes with it the dissipativity the prior leak gave the
    dithered loop; on the instance the dither's way out of the exact fit's well is a monotone climb, without a saddle,
    to a plateau of approximate fits with saturated amplitudes; the dead zone on the unconstrained coordinates
    replaces the gauge's saturation by a non-degenerate wall, so the entropic drift of
    \hyperref[res:thmDppp:37]{Proposition D$'''$.37} ends at the vertices of the representable polytope (at leading
    order: the envelope is $C^{1,1}$, so the drift's description has a boundary layer at the faces), and keeps
    \hyperref[res:thmDppp:38]{Proposition D$'''$.38}'s selection of $W^\sharp$; it leaves the deterministic objects
    inside the box unchanged, not the stationary averages, which weigh the plateau where it meets the box. Numerics:
    \code{exp_gauge_plateau.py}. Verdict: classical ingredients; the loss of dissipativity under the cohesion leak and
    its consequences for the gauge are new.
  \item \emph{Proposition D$'''$.41 (measurement noise on the gauge).} That isotropic or label noise near a manifold of
    minimisers produces a drift along it, computed by Katzenberger's theorem, is Li--Wang--Arora's (\cite{S64}), the
    isotropic noise's log-determinant drift among it; that label noise drawn afresh at each step selects flat minima
    (the trace of the Gauss--Newton matrix), with a strength set by step $\times$ variance and, at a finite step, the
    regulariser $-(1/2\eta)\tr\log(I - \eta\nabla^2L/2)$, is Blanc--Gupta--Valiant--Valiant's and Damian--Ma--Lee's, the
    noise covariance's shape mattering HaoChen--Wei--Lee--Ma's, the singular limits of noise injection
    Shalova--Schlichting--Peletier's; that the white limit of physical noise is Stratonovich's, that It\^o's and
    Stratonovich's drifts differ by the noise-induced term, and that a coloured noise's limit is It\^o's, Stratonovich's
    or neither according to its correlation time against the relaxation time, is classical (Wong--Zakai, van Kampen,
    Horsthemke--Lefever, Kupferman--Pavliotis--Stuart, Pavliotis--Stuart); the continuum filter $1/(1 + k\tau)$ is the
    active Ornstein--Uhlenbeck particle's effective temperature and $\det(I + \tau\nabla^2U)$ the unified coloured-noise
    approximation's factor (Jung--H\"anggi, Maggi et al.); SGD's SDE models are It\^o's (Li--Tai--E, Li--Malladi--Arora);
    correlated and anti-correlated noise act on the Hessian's trace, a weighted trace or the weight variances (Orvieto et
    al., Lucchi et al., Beneventano, K\"uhn--Rosenow, and an abstract by Kim et al.\ with a correlation-time trade-off);
    the examples' It\^o laws are Ziyin--Li--Ueda's law of balance and Ziyin--Wang--Li--Wu's noise equilibria, the
    diagonal network's label-noise Lasso Pillaud-Vivien--Reygner--Flammarion's (\cite{S69}). What is specific here: for
    noise entering through the error, the identity that the Wong--Zakai term cancels Katzenberger's It\^o drift exactly on
    the manifold --- from the antisymmetry the limit map's invariance imposes on
    $\langle\nabla r\otimes\nabla(\partial\Phi\cdot U)\rangle$ --- and its extension to smooth noise of any temporal
    spectrum; the coloured filter of label noise's drift along $M$ --- the increment's second moment, the cross term
    $\langle\nabla\psi\cdot n\xi\rangle$ cancelling by the same antisymmetry ---, which contains Damian--Ma--Lee's
    finite-step regulariser as its white case and passes to $(1/\tau_c)\log\det(I + \tau_c\nabla^2J)$ for a slow Markov
    noise, a concave potential for every colour, a slow Markov noise acting as a dither of the flow's own mobility; the
    closed forms on the replicator's masses, with their coordinate dependence, and the coloured selection's
    multistability there. We did not find the cancellation or the coloured filter of the drift along $M$ stated.
    Numerics: \code{exp_gauge_label.py}. Verdict: classical ingredients, a new exact identity with a practical
    consequence (continuous-time adaptation does not select through its measurement noise) and a new law for correlated
    label noise (its colour sets the implicit regulariser, concave and multistable when coloured).
  \item \emph{Proposition D$'''$.42 (several particles per class).} The change to a cluster's barycentre and internal
    (Jacobi) coordinates, with unit Jacobian, the block structure of a translation-invariant interaction, and the
    non-Gibbs stationary law of a linear system driven by a noise not proportional to its mobility are classical; that
    the positions' Hessian at an exact fit factors into the barycentres' fit part and the internal cohesion part follows.
    What is specific here: the consequence for the replicator's gauge --- under the isotropic dither the log-determinant
    selection and its Fixman correction act on the class masses exactly as with one particle per class, with $m$ the
    number of particles, and the within-class fractions neither drift at Katzenberger's order nor diffuse at any
    $\gamma$; an anisotropic dither makes them drift at $O(\gamma\tau)$ and moves the class masses off the per-class law
    at unequal splits; under measurement noise the equal split is an invariant subspace carrying the one-per-class loop,
    and the cohesion breaks the cancellation elsewhere. Numerics: \code{exp_gauge_classes.py}. Verdict: a robustness
    result closing an open item, with its limits.
  \item \emph{Proposition D$'''$.43 (the colour of the measurement noise selects the kernels).} That label noise drawn
    afresh selects a weighted $\ell^1$ norm on quadratic parametrisations --- and so recovers sparse ground truths under
    $\ell^1$'s conditions ---, and at a finite $\sigma$ a Lasso, is HaoChen--Wei--Lee--Ma's and
    Pillaud-Vivien--Reygner--Flammarion's; that a finite step turns the selected trace into a strictly convex
    regulariser, Damian--Ma--Lee's; that the hyperbolic entropy's interpolant interpolates between the kernel and the
    rich regimes, Woodworth et al.'s; that sparse Bayesian learning's cost
    $\log\det(\lambda I + \Phi\Gamma\Phi^\T) + y^\T(\lambda I + \Phi\Gamma\Phi^\T)^{-1}y$ keeps its global minimum at the
    sparsest solution with few local minima, Tipping's and Wipf--Rao's, and that its non-separable reweighting succeeds
    where $\ell^1$ fails on a set of positive measure, Wipf--Nagarajan's; that $\log\det$ is a concave surrogate of rank
    minimised by reweighted trace steps, Fazel--Hindi--Boyd's, and reweighted $\ell^1$ Cand\`es--Wakin--Boyd's; that
    gradient noise attracts sparse invariant sets, Chen et al.'s; that Fisher--Rao and Wasserstein--Fisher--Rao particle
    flows solve sparse problems on measures, Chizat's; the dual certificate for $\ell^1$, Fuchs' and Tropp's
    (\cite{S69}, \cite{S70}, \cite{S72}). What is specific here: that the temporal correlation of the label noise sets the
    sparsity prior of the explicit (Euler) update's selection along the exact fits --- the trace for white noise, Fazel's
    log-det heuristic on $\Phi\operatorname{diag}\abs w\Phi^\T$ for a slow Markov noise (sparse Bayesian learning's
    log-det term at $\gamma\propto\abs w$ and $\lambda = 1/\tau_c$, not its penalty: Wipf--Rao's results on its local
    minima do not transfer), concave for every colour in the continuum --- through
    \hyperref[res:thmDppp:41]{Proposition D$'''$.41}'s filter; the stability law of sparse fits under it (a weighted
    certificate, sufficient, $\propto1/\tau_c$ beyond a correlation time that grows with the dictionary's coherence); and
    that this selection recovers sparse kernel expansions where basis pursuit fails --- the reduced flow, and label-noise
    descent at small $\sigma$ ---, as Wipf--Nagarajan's theorem leads one to expect, at rates sparse Bayesian learning
    itself matches or beats. We did not find it stated. Numerics: \code{exp_colour_selection.py}. Verdict: a new
    mechanism --- the noise's spectrum as the sparsity prior of a sampled-data adaptation, at a rate $\propto h\sigma^2$
    (none in continuous time; Heun's scheme removes most of it) ---, not a better sparse solver; for self-adaptive kernel
    networks with an explicit update a slow measurement noise prunes the dictionary, slowly (millions of updates here).
  \item \emph{Proposition D$'''$.45 (the free mass--amplitude law as a mirror flow).} In Proposition D$'''$.44's flat
    coordinates the free law is the Euclidean gradient flow of a separable $2\to1$ reparametrisation, so its being a
    mirror flow with a start-dependent potential is Li--Wang--Lee--Arora's theorem, with one conserved function per unit
    (Marcotte--Gribonval--Peyr\'e; Azulay et al.'s $u\odot v$); the kernel and rich regimes and the $\ell^2$-to-entropy
    shape (Woodworth et al.; Ghai--Hazan--Singer), exponentiated gradient as mirror descent (Kivinen--Warmuth;
    Amid--Warmuth), the invariance of stochastic mirror descent's limit under noise in the dual span (Gunasekar et al.;
    Azizan--Hassibi), SGD's It\^o noise shrinking the effective initialisation (Pesme--Pillaud-Vivien--Flammarion; Even et
    al.) and isotropic label noise not selecting (HaoChen--Wei--Lee--Ma), and Bregman Lyapunov functions for
    mirror-descent adaptation laws (Boffi--Slotine) are classical (\cite{S72}). What is specific here: the paradigm's
    saturating unit made explicit --- the conserved $Q$, the mirror coordinate in every regime, the conjugate as the mass
    gained, the saturation's soft wall and entropy tail, the rich limit's weights $1/\sqrt{\beta^0}$ (a power law in the
    starting mass) with their concave correction, the branches ---; the Euler update's per-step change of $Q$ identified
    with the conserved share of Proposition D$'''$.44's selection, and the mirror update that removes it. Numerics:
    \code{exp_mirror_free.py}. Verdict: a classical mechanism made explicit for the paradigm's law, with design
    consequences --- the starting masses as a sparsity prior (paid for in time), the integrator as a switch for the
    noise's selection.
  \item \emph{Proposition D$'''$.46 (a leak in the mirror coordinates).} A leak in the dual coordinates of a
    continuous-time mirror flow, its regulariser-perturbed rest point, its Bregman (Fenchel-coupling) Lyapunov function
    and the vanishing-leak selection are established (Gao--Pavel; Romano--Pavel; Coucheney--Gaujal--Mertikopoulos for
    the replicator; P\'erolat et al.; Abe et al.; Alvarez--Bolte--Brahic), as are the $\sigma$-modification's ultimate
    bound (Ioannou--Kokotovi\'c; Ioannou--Sun), the averaging of adaptive systems with the filtered regressor (Anderson et
    al.; Bodson et al.; Sastry--Bodson), the LASSO and its irrepresentable condition (Tibshirani; Zhao--Yu; Wainwright)
    and mirror-descent adaptation laws (Boffi--Slotine) (\cite{S73}). What is specific here: the leak realised on the
    paradigm's $(\alpha,\beta)$ law with the conservation law kept, its ultimate bound in the adaptive loop with this law,
    the averaged equilibrium with the error filter's Gram as a monotone equation for this law, and the starting masses
    setting the regime --- a ridge, or a LASSO with penalty $\sigma\lambda_1\propto\sigma/\sqrt{\beta^0}$ that keeps the
    target's kernels under the irrepresentable condition. Numerics: \code{exp_mirror_leak.py}. Verdict: classical
    ingredients assembled into a robust version of the paradigm's mass law, whose small-start limit selects the target's
    kernels under the (weighted) irrepresentable condition.
  \item \emph{Proposition D$'''$.47 (the free law's branches).} Exponentiated gradient and its Bregman-projection bias
    (Kivinen--Warmuth; Gunasekar et al.), Hessian--Riemannian gradient flows on constrained domains
    (Alvarez--Bolte--Brahic), nonnegative least squares by overparametrised gradient flow (Chou--Maly--Verdun et al.),
    the hyperbolic entropy of $u^2 - v^2$ (Woodworth et al.; Ghai--Hazan--Singer), the uniqueness of nonnegative sparse
    solutions and nonnegative super-resolution without separation (Donoho--Tanner; Bruckstein--Elad--Zibulevsky;
    Schiebinger--Robeva--Recht; Eftekhari et al.) and the exponential penalty in linear programming (Cominetti--San
    Mart\'in; Weed) are classical (\cite{S74}). What is specific here: the paradigm's own law becomes an exponentiated
    gradient on its branches with a temperature read off the conservation law, so that small starts freeze signs; the
    limit for every start --- mixed free and branch particles, inexact fits --- as the Bregman projection onto the
    sign-constrained least-squares fits with the extinction of the vanishing coordinates (completing
    \hyperref[res:thmDppp:45]{D$'''$.45}(iii)); $B(0,w) = \beta/(\gamma m)$; the two rich limits' temperatures; and the
    scale-independent selection of a positive target's kernels. Numerics: \code{exp_mirror_branch.py}. Verdict: classical
    mechanisms identified in the paradigm's law, closing its implicit-bias picture for every start.
  \item \emph{Proposition D$'''$.48 (the rich transit).} The inverse scale space flow and its adaptive algorithm
    (Burger--Gilboa--Osher--Xu; Burger--M\"oller--Benning--Osher), saddle-to-saddle dynamics of diagonal linear networks at
    vanishing initialisation and their identification with the adaptive inverse scale space method (Pesme--Flammarion),
    incremental learning (Berthier; Gissin--Shalev-Shwartz--Daniely), inverse scale space limits of mirror flows by Mosco
    convergence (Berthier--Pillaud-Vivien, 2026), depth-$D$ potentials and polynomial scales (Woodworth et al.; Jacot et
    al.; Chou--Maly--Rauhut), sliding Frank--Wolfe and ADCG, conic particle gradient descent, condensation and early
    alignment at small initialisation, and alternating gradient flows are known (\cite{S75}). What is specific here: the
    paradigm's free mass--amplitude unit as an effective depth-3 unit with an explicit potential, so that its transit has
    the power scale $(\text{mass})^{-1/2}$ from zero amplitudes and the logarithmic one on its branches --- a continuum in
    between, set by $\alpha(0)/(\text{mass})^{1/2}$ ---; the sliding transit with the paradigm's moving centres (the
    inactive kernels frozen by the mass in the position law's drift, the creep's profile and the margin condition
    $\varepsilon\ll\mu^3$, ties shared); and the positions' metric deciding where the massless kernels go (frozen, or
    condensed at the correlation's peaks) and how small the masses must be for one kernel per feature --- not the limit's
    count, which is one per feature under both (round 37 refuted the dichotomy of counts). Unbalanced initialisations
    (Kunin et al.\ 2024), deep diagonal networks as mirror flows (Labarri\`ere et al.\ 2024) and feature quantisation at
    small initialisation (Maennel--Bousquet--Gelly 2018) are related. Numerics: \code{exp_mirror_transit.py}. Verdict:
    the limit theorem Berthier--Pillaud-Vivien's route for an $\varepsilon$-dependent family, (ii) their entropic case;
    the sliding transit, as a gradient-flow limit, apparently new (formal).
\end{itemize}

\begin{proof}
\emph{Step 0 (a priori bounds).} For either system,
$\frac{\dd}{\dd s}\norm{\vartheta - \vartheta^0}^2_{\Ginv}
= 2\ip{\vartheta - \vartheta^0}{\langle\nabla_\vartheta(r^\T\tilde\Phi(\cdot\,;\vartheta))\rangle_\rho}
- 2\sigma_0\norm{\vartheta - \vartheta^0}^2_{\Ginv}$ with
$\abs{\ip{\vartheta - \vartheta^0}{\langle\nabla_\vartheta(r^\T\tilde\Phi)\rangle_\rho}}
= \abs{\langle r^\T\nabla_\vartheta\tilde\Phi(\vartheta - \vartheta^0)\rangle_\rho}
\le\langle\norm{r}\rangle_\rho L_\vartheta^G\norm{\vartheta - \vartheta^0}_{\Ginv}
\le(F_\rho + K_\Phi)L_\vartheta^G\norm{\vartheta - \vartheta^0}_{\Ginv}$, so
$D^+\norm{\vartheta - \vartheta^0}_{\Ginv}\le L_\vartheta^G(F_\rho + K_\Phi) - \sigma_0\norm{\vartheta - \vartheta^0}_{\Ginv}$
and $\norm{\vartheta(s) - \vartheta^0}_{\Ginv}\le R_\Theta$; hence $\norm{\delta_i}_{\Ginv}\le2R_\Theta$,
$D\le D_{\max}$, and
$\norm{\dd\vartheta/\dd s}_{\Ginv} = \norm{G^{1/2}\langle\nabla_\vartheta(r^\T\tilde\Phi)\rangle_\rho - \sigma_0G^{-1/2}(\vartheta - \vartheta^0)}\le V_{\max}$.
Two Lipschitz facts: for $\Theta$, $\bar\Theta$ with rows $\vartheta_j$, $\bar\vartheta_j$ and
$A_j(x) := \int_0^1\nabla_\vartheta\tilde\Phi(x;\bar\vartheta_j + t\delta_j)\dd t$,
\begin{gather}
  \fhat_\Theta - \fhat_{\bar\Theta} = \frac1m\sum_jA_j\delta_j =: h,\qquad
  \norm{h}_{L^2(\rho)}\le L_\vartheta^G\frac1m\sum_j\norm{\delta_j}_{\Ginv}\le L_\vartheta^GD^{1/2},
  \tag{0.1}\\
  \begin{split}
  &\norm{R^G[\mu_{\bar\Theta}](\vartheta) - R^G[\mu_s](\vartheta)}\le L_2^G\norm{r_{\bar\Theta} - r_{\mu_s}}_{L^1(\rho)}\le L_2^GY,\\
  &\norm{(\nabla_\vartheta\tilde\Phi(x;\vartheta_i) - A_i(x))v}\le\frac{L_2^G}2\norm{\delta_i}_{\Ginv}\norm{v}_{\Ginv}.
  \end{split}
  \tag{0.2}
\end{gather}

\smallskip
\emph{Step 1 (the identity).} Since $\dd\delta_i/\dd s = -G[\nabla\mathcal{V}[\mu_\Theta](\vartheta_i)
- \nabla\mathcal{V}[\mu_s](\bar\vartheta_i)]$ and $\ip{\delta}{Gv}_{\Ginv} = \ip{\delta}{v}$,
\begin{gather*}
  \tfrac12\frac{\dd D}{\dd s} = -\frac1m\sum_i\ip{\delta_i}{\nabla\mathcal{V}[\mu_\Theta](\vartheta_i) - \nabla\mathcal{V}[\mu_s](\bar\vartheta_i)}
  = T_A + T_B + T_C,\\
  T_A := -\frac1m\sum_i\ip{\delta_i}{\nabla\mathcal{V}[\mu_{\bar\Theta}](\vartheta_i) - \nabla\mathcal{V}[\mu_{\bar\Theta}](\bar\vartheta_i)},\\
  T_B := -\frac1m\sum_i\ip{\delta_i}{\nabla V_L[\mu_\Theta](\vartheta_i) - \nabla V_L[\mu_{\bar\Theta}](\vartheta_i)},\\
  T_C := -\frac1m\sum_i\ip{\delta_i}{\nabla V_L[\mu_{\bar\Theta}](\bar\vartheta_i) - \nabla V_L[\mu_s](\bar\vartheta_i)}
\end{gather*}
(the leak is linear and sits in $T_A$; the three brackets telescope to
$\nabla\mathcal{V}[\mu_\Theta](\vartheta_i) - \nabla\mathcal{V}[\mu_s](\bar\vartheta_i)$). The self term $T_A$
compares the \emph{reference's} potential at the two points; the interaction term $T_B$ compares the two empirical
measures at the $m$-system's point; $T_C$ is the reference's sampling error.
\code{chaos_learning.AveragedSlowLoop.decomposition} evaluates the three terms and checks the identity to rounding.

\smallskip
\emph{Step 2 (the self term is local convexity).}
$\nabla\mathcal{V}[\mu_{\bar\Theta}](\vartheta_i) - \nabla\mathcal{V}[\mu_{\bar\Theta}](\bar\vartheta_i)
= \int_0^1\nabla^2\mathcal{V}[\mu_{\bar\Theta}](\bar\vartheta_i + t\delta_i)\dd t\cdot\delta_i
= \sigma_0\Ginv\delta_i - \bar R_i\delta_i$ with $\bar R_i := \int_0^1R[\mu_{\bar\Theta}](\bar\vartheta_i + t\delta_i)\dd t$,
so $T_A = -\sigma_0D + \frac1m\sum_i\ip{\delta_i}{\bar R_i\delta_i}$ and
$\ip{\delta}{R\delta}\le\lambda_{\max}(R^G)\norm{\delta}^2_{\Ginv}$. If $\norm{\delta_i}_{\Ginv}\le\varrho'$ the
segment lies in $N_{\varrho'}(\supp\mu_s)$ ($\bar\vartheta_i\in\supp\mu_s$), and by (0.2)
$\lambda_{\max}(R^G[\mu_{\bar\Theta}])\le\kappa_R[\mu_s](N_{\varrho'}(\supp\mu_s)) + L_2^GY$ there. Hence, as long
as $\max_i\norm{\delta_i}_{\Ginv}\le\varrho'$,
\begin{equation}
  T_A\le-\bigl(a(s) - L_2^GY(s)\bigr)D.
  \tag{2.1}
\end{equation}

\smallskip
\emph{Step 3 (the interaction term is a negative square at linear order).}
\[
  \nabla V_L[\mu_\Theta](\vartheta_i) - \nabla V_L[\mu_{\bar\Theta}](\vartheta_i)
  = \langle\nabla_\vartheta\tilde\Phi(\cdot\,;\vartheta_i)^\T(\fhat_\Theta - \fhat_{\bar\Theta})\rangle_\rho
  = \langle\nabla_\vartheta\tilde\Phi(\cdot\,;\vartheta_i)^\T h\rangle_\rho,
\]
so, with the $L^2(\rho;\R^n)$ inner product $\langle\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\rangle$,
\begin{equation}
  \begin{split}
  T_B &= -\Bigl\langle\Bigl\langle\frac1m\sum_i\nabla_\vartheta\tilde\Phi(\cdot\,;\vartheta_i)\delta_i,\,h\Bigr\rangle\Bigr\rangle
  = -\norm{h}^2 - \Bigl\langle\Bigl\langle\frac1m\sum_i(\nabla_\vartheta\tilde\Phi(\cdot\,;\vartheta_i) - A_i)\delta_i,\,h\Bigr\rangle\Bigr\rangle\\
  &\le-\norm{h}^2 + \norm{h}\frac{L_2^G}2D\le\frac{(L_2^G)^2D^2}{16}
  \end{split}
  \tag{3.1}
\end{equation}
by (0.2) and $\max_x(-x^2 + cx) = c^2/4$. The leading part
$-\norm{h}^2 = -\norm{\fhat_\Theta - \fhat_{\bar\Theta}}^2_{L^2(\rho)}$ is the flat convexity of $\bar L$: the
interaction contracts, and only the Taylor remainder of the features, quadratic in $D$, is charged. This is the step
that Theorem D$''$'s small-gain bound replaces by $+L_\vartheta^2D$-type terms.

\smallskip
\emph{Step 4 (the sampling term).}
$\nabla V_L[\mu_{\bar\Theta}](\bar\vartheta_i) - \nabla V_L[\mu_s](\bar\vartheta_i)
= \langle\nabla_\vartheta\tilde\Phi(\cdot\,;\bar\vartheta_i)^\T(\fhat_{\bar\Theta} - \fhat[\mu_s])\rangle_\rho$, so
$\abs{\ip{\delta_i}{\cdot}}\le L_\vartheta^G\norm{\delta_i}_{\Ginv}Y$ and
\begin{equation}
  T_C\le L_\vartheta^GD^{1/2}Y.
  \tag{4.1}
\end{equation}

\smallskip
\emph{Step 5 (the differential inequalities).} While $\max_i\norm{\delta_i}_{\Ginv}\le\varrho'$,
\begin{equation}
  \tfrac12\frac{\dd D}{\dd s}\le-(a - L_2^GY)D + \frac{(L_2^G)^2D^2}{16} + L_\vartheta^GD^{1/2}Y,
  \tag{5.1}
\end{equation}
and per particle, from the same three brackets with
$\abs{T_{B,i}}\le\norm{\delta_i}L_\vartheta^G\norm{h}\le\norm{\delta_i}(L_\vartheta^G)^2D^{1/2}$ by (0.1) and
$\abs{T_{C,i}}\le\norm{\delta_i}L_\vartheta^GY$,
\begin{equation}
  D^+\norm{\delta_i}_{\Ginv}\le-(a - L_2^GY)\norm{\delta_i}_{\Ginv} + (L_\vartheta^G)^2D^{1/2} + L_\vartheta^GY.
  \tag{5.2}
\end{equation}

\smallskip
\emph{Step 6 (the bootstrap on the good event).} Fix a horizon $S\le\infty$, let
$\mathcal{G}_S := \{\sup_{s\le S}Y(s)\le\theta\}$ and
$T^* := \inf\{s\le S : D(s)\ge\varrho^2\text{ or }\max_i\norm{\delta_i(s)}_{\Ginv}\ge\varrho'\}$ ($T^* > 0$ since
$\delta(0) = 0$; everything below is on $[0,S]$). On $\mathcal{G}_S$ and $[0,T^*)$: $L_2^GY\le\kappa/8$,
$(L_2^G)^2D/16\le(L_2^G)^2\varrho^2/16\le\kappa/8$, and
$L_\vartheta^GD^{1/2}Y\le(\kappa/8)D + (2(L_\vartheta^G)^2/\kappa)Y^2$ (Young), so (5.1) gives
$\dd D/\dd s\le-2(a - 3\kappa/8)D + (4(L_\vartheta^G)^2/\kappa)Y^2$. Since
$a - 3\kappa/8\ge\kappa/2 - (\kappa - a)_+$, $\int_r^s(a - 3\kappa/8)\ge(\kappa/2)(s - r) - \Lambda_1$ for $r\le s$,
and Gr\"onwall gives
\begin{equation}
  \begin{split}
  D(s)\le Z(s) &:= 4e^{2\Lambda_1}(L_\vartheta^G)^2\kappa^{-1}\int_0^se^{-\kappa(s-r)}Y(r)^2\dd r\\
  &\le4e^{2\Lambda_1}(L_\vartheta^G)^2\kappa^{-2}\theta^2\le\varrho^2/4\qquad\text{on }\mathcal{G}_S\cap[0,T^*)
  \end{split}
  \tag{6.1}
\end{equation}
by the second constraint on $\theta$. Per particle, (5.2) with $D^{1/2}\le\varrho/2$ and $Y\le\theta$, and
$\int_r^s(a - \kappa/8)\ge(\kappa/2)(s - r) - \Lambda_1$, gives
$\norm{\delta_i(s)}_{\Ginv}\le2e^{\Lambda_1}\kappa^{-1}[(L_\vartheta^G)^2\varrho/2 + L_\vartheta^G\theta]
\le\varrho'/4 + \varrho'/2 = 3\varrho'/4$ by the second constraint on $\varrho$ and the third on $\theta$. Both
thresholds are therefore strict on $[0,T^*)$, so $T^* = S$ on $\mathcal{G}_S$ by continuity: $D\le Z$ and
$\max_i\norm{\delta_i}\le3\varrho'/4$ on $[0,S]$ on $\mathcal{G}_S$. $Z$ does not involve the $m$-system.

\smallskip
\emph{Step 7 (expectation).} For $s\le S$,
\begin{multline*}
  \E D(s)\le\E[Z(s)] + D_{\max}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{G}_S^c)
  \le4e^{2\Lambda_1}(L_\vartheta^G)^2\kappa^{-1}\int_0^se^{-\kappa(s-r)}\E Y(r)^2\dd r + D_{\max}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{G}_S^c)\\
  \le C_1/m + 4R_\Theta^2\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{G}_S^c),
\end{multline*}
using $\E Y(r)^2\le K_\Phi^2/m$.

\smallskip
\emph{Step 8 (the good event).} $Y(s)$ is a function of the i.i.d.\ sample $(\vartheta_j(0))_{j\le m}$:
$Y(s) = \norm{\frac1m\sum_j[\tilde\Phi(\cdot\,;X_s(\vartheta_j(0))) - \int\tilde\Phi(\cdot\,;X_s(\vartheta))\dd\mu_0]}_{L^2(\rho)}$.
Changing one $\vartheta_j(0)$ changes it by at most $2K_\Phi/m$ (bounded differences), and
$\E Y(s)\le(\E Y^2)^{1/2}\le K_\Phi/m^{1/2}$; McDiarmid's inequality~\cite{S36} gives
$\mathbb{P}(Y(s)\ge K_\Phi/m^{1/2} + t)\le\exp(-mt^2/(2K_\Phi^2))$, so for $m\ge m_0 = 16K_\Phi^2/\theta^2$
($K_\Phi/m^{1/2}\le\theta/4$, $t = \theta/4$)
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb{P}(Y(s)\ge\theta/2)\le\exp\bigl(-m\theta^2/(32K_\Phi^2)\bigr)\qquad\text{for every fixed }s.
  \tag{8.1}
\end{equation}
In time, $\abs{Y(s) - Y(s')}\le2L_\vartheta^GV_{\max}\abs{s - s'}$ (each $X_s(\vartheta)$ moves at speed
